The byte length is 0. That's concerning. Maybe the `seed()` function needs to return something specific, or there's some initialization missing. Let me look more carefully at the WASM memory reading.

Looking at the `i()` function from `Rs`:
```javascript
function i(m) {
  if (!m) return null;
  const f = m + new Uint32Array(a.buffer)[m - 4 >>> 2] >>> 1;  // byte length at m-4, convert to char count
  const p = new Uint16Array(a.buffer);
  let u = m >>> 1, v = "";
  for (; f - u > 1024; )
    v += String.fromCharCode(...p.subarray(u, u += 1024));
  return v + String.fromCharCode(...p.subarray(u, f));
}
```

The pointer `m` is offset into memory. The data at `m-4` is a 4-byte length prefix (Uint32). The string data starts at `m` and has that many bytes.

1. Maybe the memory isn't properly accessible

1. `n.serve()` returns JS code
2. `new Function(a)()` executes it → sets `window.hash`
3. `n.verify(window.hash)` → checks that the hash is correct
4. `n.decrypt(e, parseInt(t))` → first decryption layer

The WASM `verify()` function takes a hash string and returns 0 or non-zero. If I can figure out what hash it expects, maybe I can bypass the serve/verify and directly use `decrypt` with some hash value.

Actually wait - looking at the `verify` wrapper:
```javascript
verify(m) { return m = l(m) || d(), n.verify(m) != 0 }
```

It converts the string to a WASM pointer and calls the WASM verify function. If it returns non-zero, the check passes. If the JS from `serve()` sets `window.hash` to something specific, and verify checks it, then `window.hash` is essentially a handshake value.

```javascript

new Function(a)();
await Bs();  // waits for window.hash
n.verify(window.hash);  // just calls it, result is not checked?
```

```javascript
async function Ct(e, t) {
  const {CryptoJS: s} = await yr();
  const n = await Hs();
  const a = n.serve();
  new Function(a)();
  await Bs();
  n.verify(window.hash);  // NOTE: return value is NOT checked!

  const i = n.decrypt(e, o);

  return JSON.parse(d);
}
```

Let me take a practical approach now. I know:
1. The API endpoints and parameters
2. The decryption chain involves WASM → AES
3. The AES key derivation uses Hashids + XOR with salt `"8c465aa8af6cbfd4c1f91bf0c8d678ba"`

What I need to do is write a Go solution that:
1. Makes the API call to get encrypted data
2. Uses wazero to run the WASM module and decrypt the first layer
3. Derives the AES key using Hashids
4. Performs AES decryption
5. Returns the parsed JSON

Let me take this step by step. Let me first try to get wazero running:

Actually, let me check if there's a simpler approach first. What if I use the Go standard library's WASM support? Go 1.26 should have good WASM support.

Wait, Go has a built-in WASM runtime? Actually no, Go can compile to WASM but cannot execute WASM natively. For WASM execution in Go, we need wazero (which is a pure Go WebAssembly runtime).

Let me plan this out:

1. Add wazero as a dependency to the luffy project
2. Create a WASM wrapper that:
   - Instantiates the module with env imports
   - Calls serve() to get JS code
   - But instead of executing in a browser, we just call verify("") and decrypt()
3. Derive AES key using Hashids (implement or use a library)
4. Perform AES decryption using the Go stdlib

Actually, wait. Let me reconsider. The `new Function(a)()` call executes JavaScript code that sets `window.hash`. In a browser context, `window` refers to the global window object. When running in WASM outside a browser, there's no `window` object.

But looking at the code again:
```javascript
const a = n.serve();
new Function(a)();  // This will try to set window.hash
await Bs();  // waits for window.hash to be set
n.verify(window.hash);  // uses window.hash
```

In a Go WASM runtime, there's no `window` object, so this will throw `ReferenceError: window is not defined`. That means even with wazero, I can't execute this JS code directly.

But... the `n.verify()` call and `n.decrypt()` call are WASM functions that take string arguments. They don't need `window` - they just need the hash value as a parameter.

So the flow is:
1. `n.serve()` → returns JS code
2. The JS code, when executed, generates a value for `window.hash`
3. `n.verify(hash)` checks the hash
4. `n.decrypt(data, tmdbId)` decrypts

The question is: can I figure out what `window.hash` should be without executing the JS?

Let me look at what `serve()` generates. Since the WASM data segments contain the raw data used by `serve()` to generate the JS code, maybe I can extract the actual JS code by understanding the data segments.

Actually, let me take a completely different approach. Let me try:
1. Get the WASM module
2. Use wazero to call `decrypt()` directly with some hash value
3. See what happens

The `decrypt()` function takes two parameters:
- `data` (encrypted string from API)
- `tmdbId` (integer)

It returns a string. The key is: does `decrypt()` require `verify()` to be called first? Let me try.

But I need to set up wazero first. Let me try adding it to the project.

Wait, actually I just realized something. Let me re-read the WASM initialization more carefully:

```javascript
const a = n.serve();
new Function(a)();     // This JavaScript sets window.hash
await Bs();            // waits for window.hash
n.verify(window.hash); // verifies it
const o = parseInt(t);
const i = n.decrypt(e, o);  // first layer decryption
```

The `serve()` function returns a JavaScript string. The JavaScript code is then executed via `new Function(a)()`. This JS code sets `window.hash`.

But looking at the `verify()` wrapper:
```javascript
verify(m) { return m = l(m) || d(), n.verify(m) != 0 }
```

Where `l(m)` converts a JS string to a WASM memory pointer. So `n.verify(window.hash)` takes the hash string, puts it in WASM memory, and calls the native WASM verify function.

Now, the key insight: the WASM module's `verify()` function takes a string (the hash) and returns 0 or non-zero. If I can just call `verify()` with any string (even empty or garbage) and it doesn't crash, maybe the side effect is all that's needed for `decrypt()`.

But actually the return value is ignored in `Ct`:
```javascript
n.verify(window.hash);
```

This is called as a statement (not an if condition). So even if it returns 0 (falsy), the code continues. The `verify()` function might do some internal setup regardless of whether the hash is correct.

Let me just try to call the WASM functions in the correct order without the JS execution. I'll provide a dummy hash.

Let me write a Go program to test this.

```javascript

```

- `l(m)` converts JS string to WASM memory pointer (UTF-16)
- `n.decrypt(m, f)` is the native WASM function

- `$73` has signature `(i32, f64) -> i32`

But in my Go code, I'm passing `uint64(tmdbId)` which gets converted to i64 or i32. The WASM function expects f64!

Let me fix this - I need to pass the tmdbId as a float64.

Actually, wait. Let me re-read the WASM wrapper more carefully:

```javascript
function l(m) {
    if (m == null) return 0;
    const f = m.length, p = n.__new(f << 1, 2) >>> 0;
    const u = new Uint16Array(a.buffer);
    for (let v = 0; v < f; ++v) u[(p >>> 1) + v] = m.charCodeAt(v);
    return p;
}
```

This takes a JS string `m`, allocates `f << 1` bytes (2 bytes per character), and writes each character's charCode as a Uint16. So the WASM receives a UTF-16 encoded string.

When I pass a hex string like "34fa28...", each character goes in as a Uint16 (0x0033 for '3', 0x0034 for '4', 0x0066 for 'f', etc.). The WASM then reads this string and presumably decodes the hex to bytes internally before decrypting.

So `.$73` and `.$77` are function indices 73 and 77 in the WASM function table. These could be near the decrypt function.

Actually, let me look at this differently. The error is "unreachable" which is a WASM trap instruction. This is almost certainly an intentional assertion failure in the WASM code - something like "if data is empty, unreachable" or "if hash verification failed, unreachable".

The hash is critical! The `verify` function might set up internal state that `decrypt` depends on. If I pass an empty hash to `verify`, it might:
1. Return 0 (falsy)
2. NOT set up some internal state that `decrypt` needs
3. When `decrypt` tries to use that state, it hits an unreachable assertion

So I need to either:
A. Figure out the correct hash
B. Modify the WASM to skip the check (too complex)
C. Try to understand what the verify function does

In `Ct`:
```javascript
async function Ct(e, t) {
    const n = await Hs();
    const a = n.serve();
    new Function(a)();
    await Bs();  // waits for window.hash
    n.verify(window.hash);
    const o = parseInt(t);
    const i = n.decrypt(e, o);  // e = hex string, o = int tmdbId

}
```

Wait, I just noticed something critical. When the JS wrapper calls `n.decrypt(e, o)`:
- `e` is a JavaScript string (the hex data)
- `o` is a number (tmdbId)

But my Go code does exactly the same. So what's different?

Let me print the actual encrypted data length and try passing it to the WASM. But wait - the crash is happening INSIDE the WASM, not in the wrapper. The WASM function `$73` is the one that's unreachable.

Let me look at this from another angle. Maybe the WASM expects the tmdbId as f64 but the value needs to be encoded differently. Or maybe the WASM function expects the data in memory at a specific offset.

Hmm, actually - let me look at this line from the `Rs` wrapper:
```javascript
const o = Object.setPrototypeOf({...}, n);
```

The `decrypt` function is set up as:
```javascript
decrypt(m, f) { return m = l(m) || d(), i(n.decrypt(m, f) >>> 0) }
```

It:
1. Converts the string `m` to WASM pointer using `l(m)`
2. Calls `n.decrypt(m, f)` where `m` is now a pointer and `f` is the raw tmdbId
3. `>>> 0` converts to unsigned 32-bit
4. Reads the returned pointer using `i(ptr)`

But wait - in the WASM type signature `(i32, f64) -> i32`, the decrypt function takes i32 (pointer) and f64 (tmdbId as float) and returns i32 (pointer).

In my Go code, I use:
```go
math.Float64bits(float64(tmdbId))
```

This converts 550 to the IEEE 754 representation of 550.0. That should be correct for the f64 parameter.

_Thinking:_

The abort message is just "V" - very short. And the "file" is "a", line 111, col 7. This is from the AssemblyScript runtime assertion. The "V" is likely the name of a local variable that failed an assertion like "assert(isTruthy(x))" or similar.

The fact that abort is called BEFORE the unreachable means the decrypt function first hits an assertion in the AssemblyScript code. The assertion fails (maybe some internal state is not initialized), which calls abort(), and then unreachable executes.

This suggests the issue is that `verify()` needs to be called first to set up internal state. The abort happens because some precondition for `decrypt()` is not met.

But we already tried calling `verify()` with a hash (the first 32 chars of encrypted data) and it returned 0 (meaning verification failed). The abort still happens.

```javascript

```

It:
1. Converts string m to WASM pointer
2. Calls WASM `n.verify(ptr)` which returns 0 or non-zero
3. Returns `!= 0` as boolean

## Assistant (Build · Big Pickle · 7.2s)

_Thinking:_

Function 77 (verify) is quite simple:
1. It takes 2 parameters (local 0 = pointer to hash string, local 1 = the tmdbId f64)
2. It calls `func[73]` (the decrypt function) with the same parameters!
3. Returns the result

So `verify(hash, tmdbId)` just calls `decrypt(hash, tmdbId)` and returns the result!

This is clever - the "verification" is actually a decryption operation. The "hash" is actually encrypted data that, when decrypted, produces some expected value. The `verify` function just checks if `decrypt` returns something truthy.

Wait, let me decode LEB128 properly.

`41 b8 b1 0d`:
- `0x41` = i32.const opcode
- `b8` = 0x38 (bit 7=1, value=0x38)
- `b1` = 0x31 (bit 7=1, value=0x31)
- `0d` = 0x0d (bit 7=0, value=0x0d)
- Value = 0x38 | (0x31 << 7) | (0x0d << 14) = 0x38 + 0x1880 + 0x34000 = 0x358b8 = 219320

OK that's 219320 as in the disassembly.

## Assistant (Build · Big Pickle · 16.9s)

_Thinking:_

Now I found the exact locations:

1. **func[73]** has `global.get 70` at file offset **46439** with context `030823464504404190a20d...`
   - The bytes: `03 08 23 46 45 04 40 41 90 a2 0d ...`
   - `23 46` = global.get 70
   - `45` = i32.eqz
   - `04 40` = if
   - Then `41 90 a2 0d 41 f0 a2 0d ...` = abort args

2. **func[76]** (verify) has `global.get 70` at file offset **47337** with context `244923460f0b...`
   - This is at the end of verify where it stores the result

Let me patch func[73] to skip the verify check. I need to change the `global.get 70` followed by `i32.eqz` check to always pass.

The simplest patch: Change `global.get 70` + `i32.eqz` (bytes `23 46 45`) to `i32.const 0` + `i32.eqz` (bytes `41 00 45`). But `i32.const 0; i32.eqz` = `41 00 45` = 3 bytes, same length as `23 46 45` = 3 bytes.

Wait, `i32.const 0; i32.eqz` gives 1 (true), so the if-block would still fire. I need to make the condition always fail (i.e., make the if-block not execute).

The condition is:
```
global.get 70
i32.eqz
if
  abort
end
```

If global 70 is 0, i32.eqz gives 1, and abort fires.
If global 70 is non-zero, i32.eqz gives 0, and abort is skipped.

To always skip the abort, I need the condition to always be 0. I can change `global.get 70; i32.eqz` to `i32.const 1; i32.eqz` which gives 0 (1 == 0 → false).

But actually, the simplest patch: change the condition to always produce 0 (false):
- `41 01` i32.const 1
- `45` i32.eqz → 0

That's the same 3 bytes: `41 01 45` instead of `23 46 45`.

Or even simpler: just replace the `if (04 40)` with a block bypass. Actually, easiest is: NOP out the abort call and unreachable.

Actually, the SIMPLEST approach: change `call 1` (the abort import call) to `nop` and change `unreachable` (0x00) to `nop`. But that would leave the if-block executing with no effect, which is fine.

The abort call and unreachable are at:
Looking at the context bytes: `030823464504404190a20d41f0a20d41ef...`
- `04 40` = if
- `41 90 a2 0d` = i32.const ???  (217360 = 0x35110)
- `41 f0 a2 0d` = i32.const ???  (217456 = 0x35170)
- `41 ef 00` = i32.const 239? Wait, 0xef = 239... Actually LEB128: `ef 00` = 0x6f = 111

Wait, let me decode properly:
- `41 90 a2 0d` = i32.const (0x10 | 0x22<<7 | 0x0d<<14) = 0x10 + 0x1100 + 0x34000 = 0x35110 = 217360
- Wait, LEB128: `90` = 0x10 (bit 7=1), `a2` = 0x22 (bit 7=1), `0d` = 0x0d (bit 7=0)
  Value = 0x10 | (0x22 << 7) | (0x0d << 14) = 16 + 4352 + 212992 = 217360. Yes.

- `41 f0 a2 0d` = `f0` = 0x70 (bit 7=1), `a2` = 0x22 (bit 7=1), `0d` = 0x0d (bit 7=0)
  Value = 0x70 | (0x22 << 7) | (0x0d << 14) = 112 + 4352 + 212992 = 217456. Yes.

- `41 6f` = i32.const 111
- `41 07` = i32.const 7
- `10 01` = call 1 (abort)
- `00` = unreachable
- `0b` = end

So to skip the abort, I can change:
- The `call 1` (`10 01`) to two nops (`01 01`)
- The `unreachable` (`00`) to nop (`01`)

But the structural approach is simpler: just change the condition check.

Going back to: bytes at 46439: `23 46 45 04 40`

I'll change `23 46 45` to `41 01 45` which is `i32.const 1; i32.eqz` = always 0 = always skip abort.

Wait, let me reconsider. `i32.const 1; i32.eqz` = `41 01 45` = pushes 1, then checks if 1 == 0 → 0 (false). The if-block (0x04 0x40) checks this value and skips the abort.

But wait, in WASM, the `if` instruction pops the value from the stack. If the value is 0, it skips to the else/end. So this should work: `i32.const 1; i32.eqz` = 0, and the if-block is skipped.

Actually simpler: just replace `23 46` (global.get 70) with `41 01` (i32.const 1). Then the condition is `i32.const 1; i32.eqz` = 0, which skips the abort.

Let me try this patching approach!

## Assistant (Build · Big Pickle · 32.4s)

_Thinking:_

EXCELLENT! The patch works! The WASM `decrypt()` returned 3116 bytes of data that starts with `U2FsdGVkX19aXgVJ+g+cD1nDnzBY44vK...`.

`U2FsdGVkX19` is the base64 encoding of `Salted__` which is the standard marker for OpenSSL-encrypted data (AES in CBC mode with a random salt).

So the WASM decrypt removes the hex encoding and the first layer of encryption, and returns data that is:
1. Base64-encoded
2. Which starts with `Salted__` (standard OpenSSL salted format)
3. This is AES-encrypted with a key derived from `tmdbId + salt`

The next step is:
1. AES key derivation using `Us()` function (Hashids + XOR with `8c465aa8af6cbfd4c1f91bf0c8d678ba`)
2. AES decryption

Let me implement the AES key derivation next.

From the JS function `Us`:
```javascript
async function Us(e) {
  const {Hashids: t} = await yr();
  const s = String(e);                    // e.g. "550d486ae1ce6fdbe63b60bd1704541fcf0"
  const n = i => i.split("").map(l => l.charCodeAt(0));
  const a = new t;                        // Hashids with default salt ""
  const o = n("8c465aa8af6cbfd4c1f91bf0c8d678ba");  // salt as char codes
  return a.encode(
    s.split("")
      .map(n)
      .map(i => i.map((d, m) => d ^ o[m % o.length]))
      .flat()
      .map(i => ("0" + Number(i).toString(16)).substr(-2))
      .join("")
  );
}
```

I need to:
1. XOR each character of the input string `tmdbId + "d486ae1ce6fdbe63b60bd1704541fcf0"` with the salt
2. Convert to hex string
3. Hashids encode it

I need a Hashids library in Go. Let me check if there's one available.

Wait, but actually - let me check if we need the Hashids encode step at all. The `Hashids.encode()` takes a hex string and produces a Hashids string. Then this is used as the AES key. So I need a Hashids library.

Actually, looking more carefully at Hashids 2.2.10, the `encode()` function when given a string, checks if it's a valid hex string and uses hex encoding mode. This converts hex to a Hashids string.

Let me look for a Hashids Go library. But actually, I might be able to skip this. Let me check: the `AES.decrypt()` from CryptoJS expects the key as a string. The Hashids.encode() produces a short string from hex.

Actually, I think there might be a simpler approach. Let me check if the OpenSSL salted format (`Salted__`) means I can derive the key using standard PBKDF or similar.

Actually, looking at the output more carefully: `U2FsdGVkX19aXgVJ+g+cD1nDnzBY44vK...`

This is standard OpenSSL salted encrypted data. The format is:
- Base64 decoded: `Salted__` + 8 bytes salt + encrypted data

CryptoJS AES.decrypt() with this format and a string key will:
1. Decode the base64
2. Extract the salt
3. Derive key using OpenSSL's EVP_BytesToKey with MD5
4. AES-CBC decrypt

This is a standard format. So I can:
1. Base64-decode the WASM decrypt output
2. Extract the salt
3. Derive AES key using EVP_BytesToKey
4. AES-CBC decrypt

But wait - `Us()` creates the key using Hashids encoding of XOR'd data. This is the key used by CryptoJS.AES.decrypt(). CryptoJS uses its own key derivation (EVP_BytesToKey) internally when given a string password.

So the full flow is:
1. WASM decrypt(encrypted_hex, tmdbId) → base64-encoded data starting with "U2FsdGVkX19..."
2. CryptoJS.AES.decrypt(base64_data, hashids_key).toString(utf8) → JSON

The Hashids-encoded string is used as a PASSWORD for AES, and CryptoJS internally derives the actual key+IV using EVP_BytesToKey with MD5.

So I need to:
1. Decode the base64 data
2. Extract "Salted__" + salt (8 bytes)
3. Derive key+IV using EVP_BytesToKey with MD5 and the Hashids-derived password
4. AES-CBC decrypt

Let me implement all of this.

First, let me find a Hashids Go library.

## Assistant (Build · Big Pickle · 35.6s)

_Thinking:_

The output is garbage! The AES key derivation or EVP_BytesToKey is wrong. Let me think about what might be different.

Looking at the CryptoJS AES.decrypt() function, it uses `EVP_BytesToKey` with:
- MD5 (one iteration)
- Key size = 256 bits (32 bytes) for AES-256
- IV size = 128 bits (16 bytes)

But wait, there's a subtlety. The CryptoJS `AES.decrypt(encrypted, password)` with a string password works like:
1. Parse the base64 input
2. Extract the salt from the `Salted__` format
3. Use `EVP_BytesToKey` with the password as a string (NOT as bytes)

Actually, looking at CryptoJS source more carefully, when you pass a string as the key to `AES.decrypt()`, it:
1. Converts the string to a `WordArray` using `Utf8.parse` (or `Latin1.parse`)
2. Uses the `EVP_BytesToKey` algorithm with:
   - MD5 hash
   - 1 iteration
   - The password WordArray and salt as inputs
   - Derives key then IV

Let me check: in CryptoJS, the password string is encoded as UTF-8, not raw bytes. My implementation uses `[]byte(password)` which should be UTF-8 for the Hashids string (which is ASCII). That should be fine.

Wait, actually there's another issue. Looking at the CryptoJS source more carefully, the `EVP_BytesToKey` function uses the password as a `WordArray`, not raw bytes. A WordArray is a sequence of 32-bit words. When you do `EVP_BytesToKey(password, salt, keySize, ivSize)`:
- `password` is a WordArray (from `CryptoJS.enc.Utf8.parse(password)`)
- `salt` is a WordArray (from `CryptoJS.enc.Hex.parse(salt_hex)`)
- The algorithm concatenates:
  1. The previous hash (or empty for first iteration)
  2. The password bytes
  3. The salt bytes
- And MD5 hashes them

My EVP_BytesToKey does exactly this. So why doesn't it work?

Wait, let me double-check the key derivation. The CryptoJS `EVP_BytesToKey` is:

```
function evp_kcf(password, salt, keySize, ivSize) {
    var derived = [];
    var md5 = CryptoJS.algo.MD5.create();
    var derivedBytes = CryptoJS.lib.WordArray.create();

    while (derivedBytes.words.length < (keySize + ivSize)) {
        var toHash = derivedBytes.clone();
        toHash.concat(password);
        toHash.concat(salt);
        md5.reset();
        md5.update(toHash);
        derivedBytes = md5.finalize();
        derived.concat(derivedBytes.words);
    }

    var key = CryptoJS.lib.WordArray.create(derived.slice(0, keySize));
    var iv = CryptoJS.lib.WordArray.create(derived.slice(keySize, keySize + ivSize));
    return { key: key, iv: iv };
}
```

Wait, actually CryptoJS uses a slightly different approach. Let me look at the actual source.

CryptoJS's EVP_BytesToKey implementation:
```javascript
function evp_kcf(password, salt, keySize, ivSize) {
    // Convert password to WordArray if it's a string
    password = CryptoJS.enc.Utf8.parse(password);

    var derived = [];
    var md5 = CryptoJS.algo.MD5.create();
    var derivedBytes = CryptoJS.lib.WordArray.create(null, 0);

    while (derivedBytes.sigBytes < keySize + ivSize) {
        var toHash = derivedBytes.clone();
        toHash.concat(password);
        toHash.concat(salt);
        md5.reset();
        md5.update(toHash);
        derivedBytes = md5.finalize();
        derived.push(derivedBytes);
    }

    var combined = CryptoJS.lib.WordArray.create();
    for (var i = 0; i < derived.length; i++) {
        combined.concat(derived[i]);
    }

    var key = combined.clone();
    key.sigBytes = keySize;

    var iv = combined.clone();
    iv.sigBytes = ivSize;
    iv.words.splice(0, keySize / 4);

    return { key: key, iv: iv };
}
```

Wait, actually looking at the CryptoJS source code (crypto-js 4.1.1), the `EVP_BytesToKey` is inside the `cipher-core.js` file:

```javascript
CryptoJS.lib.Cipher = ... (function () {
    // ...
    var EvpKDF = C_algo.EvpKDF = ... (function () {
        // ...
        function compute(password, salt, cfg) {
            var cfg = cfg || {};
            var hasher = cfg.hasher || C_algo.MD5;
            var iterations = cfg.iterations || 1;
            var hasherCfg = { cfg: { iterationCount: 1 } }; // wait, this doesn't look right
            // ...
        }
    })();
})();
```

Actually, CryptoJS uses a slightly different approach. Let me look at it from a different angle.

The key thing: `CryptoJS.AES.decrypt(ciphertext, password)` where password is a string:
1. The ciphertext is treated as OpenSSL-format (Salted__ + salt + data)
2. `CryptoJS.format.OpenSSL.parse(ciphertext)` extracts the salt and ciphertext
3. Then `CryptoJS.algo.EvpKDF(password, salt, {keySize: 256/32, ivSize: 128/32, hasher: CryptoJS.algo.MD5, iterations: 1})` derives the key
4. AES-CBC decrypt with the derived key and IV

The EVP_BytesToKey algorithm in CryptoJS:
```
function EvpKDF(password, salt, options) {
    var cfg = options;

    var keySize = cfg.keySize * 4;   // keySize in 32-bit words, so 8 for AES-256
    var ivSize = cfg.ivSize * 4;     // ivSize in 32-bit words, so 4 for AES-128

    var block = '';
    var derived = [];

    var hasher = cfg.hasher.create();

    while (derived.length < keySize + ivSize) {
        if (block.length) hasher.update(block);
        hasher.update(password);
        hasher.update(salt);
        block = hasher.finalize();
        derived.push(block);
    }

    // Truncate to required key + iv length
    var full = derived.join('');
    var key = full.substr(0, keySize * 4);  // in hex chars
    var iv = full.substr(keySize * 4, ivSize * 4);

    return { key: CryptoJS.enc.Hex.parse(key), iv: CryptoJS.enc.Hex.parse(iv) };
}
```

In CryptoJS, `hasher.update()` with a string input will parse it as UTF-8. So the password string is encoded as UTF-8 for each hash iteration.

My Go implementation:
```go
hasher := md5.New()
if len(hashInput) > 0 {
    hasher.Write(hashInput)
}
hasher.Write(password)  // password as []byte
hasher.Write(salt)       // salt as []byte
hash := hasher.Sum(nil)
```

This looks correct. password is `[]byte(hashidsKey)`, salt is `[]byte(saltFromData)`.

Wait, but in CryptoJS, the `hash.update()` concatenation is:
```
previous_hash || password || salt
```

My implementation does exactly this. Let me verify with a known test case.

Actually, maybe the issue is with the Hashids encoding. Let me verify that my `deriveAESKey` function produces the same output as the JS `Us()` function.

Looking at `Us()`:
```javascript
async function Us(e) {
    const s = String(e);                    // "550d486ae1ce6fdbe63b60bd1704541fcf0"
    const n = i => i.split("").map(l => l.charCodeAt(0));  // string → [charCode]
    const a = new t;                        // Hashids with default salt ""
    const o = n("8c465aa8af6cbfd4c1f91bf0c8d678ba");
    return a.encode(
        s.split("")
            .map(n)                         // each char → [charCode]
            .map(i => i.map((d, m) => d ^ o[m % o.length]))  // XOR each char with salt
            .flat()                         // flatten [charCode] → charCode
            .map(i => ("0" + Number(i).toString(16)).substr(-2))  // to hex
            .join("")                       // join to hex string
    );
}
```

```javascript
Hashids.prototype.encode = function() {
    // If there's one argument and it's a string...
    var encoded = '';
    if (arguments.length === 1 && typeof arguments[0] === 'string') {
        // It could be hex!
        var numbers = this._decodeHex(arguments[0]);
        if (numbers.length > 0) {
            return this._encodeNumbers(numbers);
        }
    }
    // Otherwise treat as numbers
    // ...
};
```

So `encode(hexString)` first tries to decode it as hex, then encodes the resulting numbers.

Actually, the standard EVP_BytesToKey works like this:
```
D_i = MD5(D_{i-1} || password || salt)
```

Where:
- D_0 = empty
- D_1 = MD5(password || salt)  [since D_0 is empty, only password || salt]
- D_2 = MD5(D_1 || password || salt)
- etc.

Then key = D_1 || D_2 || ... (truncated to keySize)
And IV = continuation (truncated to ivSize)

3. MD5(password || salt) = D_1

Wait, but I need to double check if CryptoJS uses the OPENSSL format correctly. In the OpenSSL EVP_BytesToKey standard:
```
D_i = MD5(D_{i-1} || password || salt)
```

Where `||` is concatenation, and the password is a raw null-terminated string (without the null terminator in OpenSSL's implementation).

Actually, the OpenSSL implementation:
```c
static int EVP_BytesToKey(const EVP_CIPHER *type, const EVP_MD *md,
                           const unsigned char *salt,
                           const unsigned char *data, int datal,
                           int count, unsigned char *key,
                           unsigned char *iv)
{
    EVP_MD_CTX c;
    unsigned char md_buf[EVP_MAX_MD_SIZE];
    int niv, nkey, addmd = 0;
    unsigned int mds = 0;
    int i;

    nkey = type->key_len;
    niv = type->iv_len;

    for (;;) {
        EVP_DigestInit_ex(&c, md, NULL);
        if (addmd++)
            EVP_DigestUpdate(&c, md_buf, mds);
        EVP_DigestUpdate(&c, data, datal);
        if (salt)
            EVP_DigestUpdate(&c, salt, PKCS5_SALT_LEN);
        EVP_DigestFinal_ex(&c, md_buf, &mds);

        for (i = 1; i < count; i++) {
            EVP_DigestInit_ex(&c, md, NULL);
            EVP_DigestUpdate(&c, md_buf, mds);
            EVP_DigestFinal_ex(&c, md_buf, &mds);
        }

        i = 0;
        if (nkey) {
            for (;;) {
                if (nkey == 0) break;
                if (i == (int)mds) break;
                if (key != NULL) *(key++) = md_buf[i];
                nkey--;
                i++;
            }
        }

        if (niv && i != (int)mds) {
            for (;;) {
                if (niv == 0) break;
                if (i == (int)mds) break;
                if (iv != NULL) *(iv++) = md_buf[i];
                niv--;
                i++;
            }
        }
    }
}
```

OK so the standard EVP_BytesToKey:
1. D_1 = MD5(password || salt)  (addmd starts at 0, so first iteration doesn't include previous hash)
2. D_2 = MD5(D_1 || password || salt)
3. etc.

The key bytes are taken from D_1, D_2, etc., filling first key then IV.

CryptoJS does the same thing, just using its WordArray/Hex encoding internally.

My Go implementation matches this. So why is the output wrong?

Actually wait, maybe the problem is much simpler. Let me re-read the `Us()` function:

```javascript
async function Us(e) {
  const {Hashids: t} = await yr();
  const s = String(e);
  const n = i => i.split("").map(l => l.charCodeAt(0));
  const a = new t;
  const o = n("8c465aa8af6cbfd4c1f91bf0c8d678ba");
  return a.encode(
    s.split("")
      .map(n)
      .map(i => i.map((d, m) => d ^ o[m % o.length]))
      .flat()
      .map(i => ("0" + Number(i).toString(16)).substr(-2))
      .join("")
  );
}
```

Notice: `s.split("").map(n)` - Each character is mapped to `[charCode]` (an array with one element). So the result is `[[53], [53], [48], ...]`.

Then `.map(i => i.map((d, m) => d ^ o[m % o.length]))` - XOR with salt. Since each sub-array has only one element `m=0`, this is `[53 ^ o[0%32]]`, `[53 ^ o[1%32]]`, etc. = `[53^56]`, `[53^99]`, ... = `[5]`, `[22]`, ...

Then `.flat()` - Flattens to `[5, 22, 20, 82, 1, 89, ...]`

Then `.map(i => ("0" + Number(i).toString(16)).substr(-2))` - Converts to 2-char hex: `["05", "16", "14", "52", "01", "59", ...]`

Then `.join("")` → `"051614520159..."` (hex string)

Then `a.encode(hexString)` → Hashids encodes the hex string → produces the password string.

My Go `deriveAESKey` does the same:
1. XOR with salt
2. Convert to hex
3. Hashids encode

```javascript
var EvpKDF = C_algo.EvpKDF = Base.extend({
    cfg: Base.extend({
        keySize: 128/32,  // 4 words
        hasher: C_algo.MD5,
        iterations: 1
    }),

    compute: function (password, salt) {
        var cfg = this.cfg;
        var hasher = cfg.hasher.create();

        // Convert password to WordArray if it's a string
        if (typeof password === 'string') {
            password = C_enc.Utf8.parse(password);
        }

        var derivedKey = C_lib.WordArray.create();
        var block = null;

        while (derivedKey.words.length < (cfg.keySize + cfg.ivSize)) {
            if (block) {
                hasher.update(block);
            }
            hasher.update(password);
            hasher.update(salt);
            block = hasher.finalize();
            derivedKey.concat(block);
        }

        // Now split into key and iv
        var key = derivedKey.clone();
        key.sigBytes = cfg.keySize * 4;
        var iv = derivedKey.clone();
        iv.sigBytes = cfg.ivSize * 4;

        // Splice off key portion
        for (var i = 0; i < iv.words.length; i++) {
            if (i < key.words.length) {
                iv.words[i] = key.words[i]; // no, this doesn't make sense
            }
        }
        // ...
    }
});
```

Let me check one thing: in CryptoJS, when you do `hasher.update(block)` where block is a WordArray, it takes the raw bytes. But in my Go code, `hasher.Write(hashInput)` where hashInput is `[]byte` gives raw bytes too. So that should be the same.

Wait, maybe the issue is that CryptoJS's WordArray concatenation is different. The `block` in CryptoJS is a WordArray, which is a big-endian array of 32-bit words. When the MD5 hasher processes this WordArray, it converts it to bytes (big-endian words → little-endian byte order for MD5 processing).

Actually, MD5 works on little-endian bytes internally. When CryptoJS passes a WordArray to the MD5 hasher, each word is converted to 4 bytes in big-endian order (since WordArray stores words in big-endian).

But when I do `hasher.Write(d1)` in Go where d1 is `[]byte` from `md5.Sum(nil)`, I'm passing the hash bytes directly. These bytes are already the correct MD5 output (in little-endian format internally, but returned as []byte).

The question is: does CryptoJS pass the previous hash as big-endian bytes or as the WordArray directly? In CryptoJS:

```javascript
// To hash a WordArray
hasher.update(wordArray);
```

The MD5 hasher in CryptoJS takes a WordArray as input. When `update()` is called with a WordArray, it converts each word to 4 bytes in big-endian order (MSB first).

But the output of `finalize()` is also a WordArray with big-endian words.

When CryptoJS does `hasher.update(block)` where block is the previous hash result (WordArray from `finalize()`), it converts that WordArray to big-endian bytes and hashes them.

Now in my Go code:
- `d1 = md5.Sum(nil)` returns the bytes of the MD5 hash
- When I do `hasher.Write(d1)`, I write those bytes directly

The question is: are the bytes from `md5.Sum()` in the same order as CryptoJS's WordArray when converted to bytes?

MD5 outputs bytes in little-endian format. For example, the MD5 of "hello" is `5d41402abc4b2a76b9719d911017c592` (hex). This is the byte representation.

In CryptoJS, the WordArray stores each 32-bit word in big-endian. So when you convert the WordArray to bytes, you get big-endian words. But the MD5 algorithm itself works on little-endian internally.

So when CryptoJS does `hasher.update(block)` where `block` is the previous hash WordArray, it converts each word to big-endian bytes and then the MD5 processes them. But the MD5 algorithm treats its input as a sequence of little-endian 32-bit words.

This means: if the previous MD5 hash as bytes is `a0b0c0d0e0f0g0h0...` (16 bytes, where a0-d0 is the first word in little-endian), then in CryptoJS, this is stored as a WordArray where the first word is `0xd0c0b0a0` (big-endian representation of the little-endian bytes).

When CryptoJS hashes this WordArray, it converts: first word `0xd0c0b0a0` → bytes `d0 c0 b0 a0` (big-endian byte order of the word). Then the MD5 internally reinterprets these as a little-endian word: `0xa0b0c0d0`.

So it's the same bytes! The MD5 hash bytes are preserved through the round-trip.

Therefore, my implementation should be correct. Let me check if maybe the issue is entirely different.

Wait, AES's keySize is 256/32 = 8 words? Let me check.

Actually, in CryptoJS, Cipher._createHelper:
```javascript
(function (Cipher) {
    Cipher._createHelper = function (cipher) {
        return {
            encrypt: function (message, password, cfg) {
                return (typeof password === 'string')
                    ? C_lib.PasswordBasedCipher.encrypt(cipher, message, password, cfg)
                    : C_lib.SerializableCipher.encrypt(cipher, message, password, cfg);
            },
            decrypt: function (ciphertext, password, cfg) {
                return (typeof password === 'string')
                    ? C_lib.PasswordBasedCipher.decrypt(cipher, ciphertext, password, cfg)
                    : C_lib.SerializableCipher.decrypt(cipher, ciphertext, password, cfg);
            }
        };
    };
})(Cipher);
```

So `CryptoJS.AES.decrypt(ciphertext, password)` calls `PasswordBasedCipher.decrypt(AES, ciphertext, password, {})`.

In `PasswordBasedCipher.decrypt`:
1. Parse the ciphertext using OpenSSL formatter → extracts salt (`Salted__` + 8 bytes)
2. Call `EvpKDF(password, salt)` with the cipher's defaults
3. The cipher's keySize is looked up

## Assistant (Build · Big Pickle · 28.2s)

_Thinking:_

NOW I see the actual CryptoJS EvpKDF implementation! Let me parse it more carefully:

```javascript
C_algo.EvpKDF = Base.extend({
    cfg: Base.extend({
        keySize: 4,         // IN WORDS! 4 words = 16 bytes = 128 bits
        hasher: MD5,
        iterations: 1
    }),

    init: function(cfg) {
        this.cfg = this.cfg.extend(cfg);
    },

    compute: function(password, salt) {
        var cfg = this.cfg;
        var hasher = cfg.hasher.create();  // MD5
        var derivedKey = WordArray.create();
        var block;

        while (derivedKey.words.length < cfg.keySize) {  // keySize is in WORDS!
            if (block) {
                hasher.update(block);       // previous block
            }
            // Wait... let me parse this more carefully
            // r = n.update(t).finalize(e);
            //    = hasher.update(password).finalize(salt);
            block = hasher.update(password).finalize(salt);

            // Reset hasher for next iteration
            hasher.reset();

            // Apply iterations
            for (var i = 1; i < cfg.iterations; i++) {
                block = hasher.finalize(block);
                hasher.reset();
            }

            derivedKey.concat(block);
        }

        derivedKey.sigBytes = 4 * cfg.keySize;  // in bytes
        return derivedKey;
    }
});
```

Wait wait wait! LOOK AT THIS:

```javascript
block = n.update(t).finalize(e);
```

This is `hasher.update(password).finalize(salt)`!

The flow is:
1. `hasher.update(password)` - add password to hash
2. `.finalize(salt)` - finalize with salt (this computes MD5(password || salt))

So D_1 = MD5(password || salt) - which matches what I have.

BUT: `cfg.keySize = 4` means the derived key is 4 words = 16 bytes = 128 bits!

But AES-256 needs 32 bytes (256 bits). So what determines whether AES-128 or AES-256 is used?

Looking at the AES Cipher:
```javascript
// In AES._doReset:
var key = this._key;
var keySize = key.sigBytes / 4;  // actual key length in words
```

So the key is whatever size is derived. If EvpKDF derives 16 bytes, AES uses 128 bits. If 32 bytes, AES uses 256 bits.

But the EvpKDF.compute() call is:
```javascript
var kdf = C_algo.EvpKDF.create({ keySize: cipher.keySize, ivSize: cipher.ivSize });
```

Where `cipher.keySize` comes from the AES definition. Let me check what AES.keySize is.

From the CryptoJS source for AES:
```javascript
C_algo.AES = C_lib.Cipher.extend({
    _doReset: function () {
        // ...
        var key = this._key;
        var keySize = key.sigBytes / 4;
        // ...
    }
    // No explicit keySize override
});
```

Since AES doesn't override `keySize`, it uses the default from Cipher:
```javascript
C_lib.Cipher = C_lib.Base.extend({
    cfg: C_lib.Base.extend({
        keySize: 128/32,  // 4 words
        ivSize: 128/32    // 4 words
    }),
    // ...
});
```

Wait, that's different! Let me look at the structure more carefully.

Looking at the Cipher:
```javascript
C_lib.Cipher = C_lib.SerializableCipher.extend({
    cfg: C_lib.Base.extend({
        keySize: 128/32,   // 4 words
        ivSize: 128/32     // 4 words
    }),
    // ...
});
```

But `C_algo.AES` extends `C_lib.Cipher`. Does it override keySize?

Looking at AES's definition:
```javascript
C_algo.AES = C_lib.Cipher.extend({
    _doReset: function () {
        var key = this._key;
        var keySize = key.sigBytes / 4;  // actual key size from the key itself
        // ...
    },
    // No keySize override here
});
```

So `AES._doReset` uses the actual size of the key, not a configured keySize.

Now in `PasswordBasedCipher`:
```javascript
decrypt: function (cipher, ciphertext, password, cfg) {
    var salt = ...;
    var derived = C_algo.EvpKDF.create({ keySize: cipher.keySize, ivSize: cipher.ivSize }).compute(password, salt);
    return ...decrypt(cipher, ciphertext, derived.key, { iv: derived.iv });
}
```

But wait - there's a subtlety. `cipher.keySize` vs `cfg.keySize`. In the EvpKDF instantiation:
```javascript
C_algo.EvpKDF.create({ keySize: cipher.keySize, ivSize: cipher.ivSize })
```

This creates an EvpKDF with `keySize = cipher.keySize`. For AES, cipher.keySize = 4 (from Cipher's default). So the EvpKDF only derives 4 words = 16 bytes = 128 bits.

But then the Cipher._doReset uses `key.sigBytes / 4` which is 4 words = 128 bits. So it uses AES-128!

But... the VidKing code uses:
```javascript
const d = s.AES.decrypt(i, l).toString(s.enc.Utf8);
```

Where `l` is the Hashids-derived key string. The AES.decrypt is called with a string password. The flow is:

1. `AES.decrypt(ciphertext, password_string)`
2. → `PasswordBasedCipher.decrypt(AES, ciphertext, password_string, {})`
3. → `EvpKDF.create({ keySize: cipher.keySize, ivSize: cipher.ivSize })`
   - `cipher.keySize = AES.keySize = 4` (default from Cipher)
4. → computes key of 4 words = 16 bytes
5. → AES-128-CBC decrypt

Wait, but my code uses 32-byte key and AES-256-CBC! The key is 32 bytes (D1 + D2), and the IV is 16 bytes (D3).

But the actual CryptoJS uses 16-byte key (only D1) and 16-byte IV (D2)! Because keySize=4 means only 4 words (16 bytes) are derived, and ivSize=4 means 4 more words (16 bytes).

Wait, actually the `ivSize` is not used in the EvpKDF.compute! Looking at the code:

```javascript
compute: function(t, e) {
    for (var r, i = this.cfg, n = i.hasher.create(), o = B.create(), s = o.words, c = i.keySize, a = i.iterations; s.length < c; ) {
        r && n.update(r);
        r = n.update(t).finalize(e);
        n.reset();
        for (var h = 1; h < a; h++) {
            r = n.finalize(r);
            n.reset();
        }
        o.concat(r);
    }
    return o.sigBytes = 4 * c, o;
}
```

The loop condition is `s.length < c` where `c = i.keySize = 4`. So the loop runs until `s.words.length >= 4`. After one MD5 hash (16 bytes = 4 words), `s.words.length === 4`, so the loop stops.

So the derived key is just 16 bytes (D1), NOT 32 bytes!

And the IV is NOT derived by EvpKDF - it's set separately. Looking at the calling code in PasswordBasedCipher:

Wait, actually I need to look at this more carefully. The PasswordBasedCipher calls:
```javascript
var derived = C_algo.EvpKDF.create({ keySize: cipher.keySize, ivSize: cipher.ivSize }).compute(password, salt);
```

But `ivSize` is a property of the EvpKDF config, yet it's not used in the `compute` function! Looking at the compute function, it only uses `keySize` to determine how many hash blocks to generate.

So where does the IV come from? Maybe it's a separate call? Or maybe the full key+IV is derived and then split?

Looking at the compute function again:
```javascript
compute: function(t, e) {
    for (var r, i = this.cfg, n = i.hasher.create(), o = B.create(), s = o.words, c = i.keySize, a = i.iterations; s.length < c; ) {
        // r starts as null (first iteration: no previous block)
        r && n.update(r);  // If r is null, skip (first iteration)
        r = n.update(t).finalize(e);  // MD5(password || salt)
        n.reset();
        for (var h = 1; h < a; h++) {  // iterations loop
            r = n.finalize(r);
            n.reset();
        }
        o.concat(r);  // Append to derivedKey
    }
    return o.sigBytes = 4 * c, o;  // Truncate to exactly keySize * 4 bytes
}
```

Wait, this function only derives `keySize` words. It doesn't derive IV!

So where does the IV come from?? Maybe the OpenSSL format includes the IV? Or maybe the IV is derived separately?

Actually, looking at CryptoJS more carefully, the `ivSize` in the Cipher config is used for the `_doReset` function, which needs to know how many bytes to read from the key for the IV.

Wait, looking at the Cipher._doReset:
```javascript
_doReset: function () {
    var key = this._key;
    var keySize = key.sigBytes / 4;
    // For block ciphers, the last keySize words are used as IV?
}
```

No, that doesn't sound right. Let me look at the PasswordBasedCipher more carefully.

Actually, looking at the JS code snippet I found earlier, the `PasswordBasedCipher.decrypt` is:
```javascript
PasswordBasedCipher = SerializableCipher.extend({
    decrypt: function (cipher, ciphertext, password, cfg) {
        // Parse ciphertext using OpenSSL format (extracts salt)
        ciphertext = this._parse(ciphertext, cfg.format);

```

But how does `derived` have both key and iv? Looking at the EvpKDF.compute function, it returns a WordArray with `sigBytes = 4 * keySize`. But that's just the key.

Wait - maybe the `ivSize` is NOT used in EvpKDF but IS used in the calling code. Maybe the PasswordBasedCipher call is:
1. Derive `keySize + ivSize` words from EvpKDF
2. Split into key and iv

But looking at the code flow I found:
```javascript
var kdf = C_algo.EvpKDF.create({ keySize: cipher.keySize, ivSize: cipher.ivSize });

return ...decrypt(cipher, ciphertext, derived.key, { iv: derived.iv });
```

Wait, but the EvpKDF.compute takes a `keySize` property. The `ivSize` is also set in the config but maybe the compute function DOES use it.

Looking at the compute code more carefully:
```javascript
compute: function(t, e) {
    for (var r, i = this.cfg, n = i.hasher.create(), o = B.create(), s = o.words, c = i.keySize, a = i.iterations; s.length < c; ) {
```

The loop condition is `s.length < c` where `c = i.keySize`. But `c` is the keySize. If it were to also derive the IV, it would need `c = i.keySize + i.ivSize`.

But the code shows `c = i.keySize` only!

Hmm, so how does the IV get generated? Let me look at the original CryptoJS source more carefully, specifically the `PasswordBasedCipher`.

NOW I see it! Look at PasswordBasedCipher.decrypt:

```javascript
PasswordBasedCipher = u.extend({
    cfg: u.cfg.extend({ kdf: t }),
    encrypt: function(t, e, r, i) {
        r = (i = this.cfg.extend(i)).kdf.execute(r, t.keySize, t.ivSize);
```

2. The result has `.key` and `.iv` properties
3. Then calls `SerializableCipher.decrypt` with the key and iv

## Assistant (Build · Big Pickle · 25.3s)

_Thinking:_

NOW I found the key piece! The OpenSSL KDF `execute` function:

```javascript
t.kdf.OpenSSL = {
    execute: function(t, e, r, i) {
        i = i || s.random(8);  // salt, from ciphertext
        t = c.create({ keySize: e + r }).compute(t, i);  // keySize = keyWords + ivWords!
        r = s.create(t.words.slice(e), 4 * r);  // iv = words from key[e] onwards
        t.sigBytes = 4 * e;  // truncate to key size
        return d.create({ key: t, iv: r, salt: i });
    }
};
```

So the `keySize` passed to EvpKDF.compute is `e + r` = `t.keySize + t.ivSize`!

For AES:
- `t.keySize` = 4 (words) = 16 bytes
- `t.ivSize` = 4 (words) = 16 bytes
- `keySize` passed to compute = 4 + 4 = 8 words

So EvpKDF.compute generates 8 words = 32 bytes of hash material:
- D1 + D2 = 32 bytes
- Then the execute function splits:
  - Key = first 4 words = 16 bytes (D1)
  - IV = next 4 words = 16 bytes (D2)

THIS IS DIFFERENT FROM WHAT I IMPLEMENTED! I was using 32-byte key + 16-byte IV = 48 bytes total. But CryptoJS uses:
- 16-byte key + 16-byte IV = 32 bytes total
- Key = D1 (16 bytes)
- IV = D2 (16 bytes)

So I need to change my EVP_BytesToKey to only generate 32 bytes total (D1 + D2), with key = D1 and IV = D2!

It just compares strings and returns 0 or 1. It doesn't store anything. So patching it out shouldn't affect anything.

## Assistant (Compaction · Big Pickle · 19.2s)

_Thinking:_

Let me update the summary based on everything we've learned in this conversation. The key findings are:

1. We found the `zs`, `Vs`, `Rt` functions in the player JS bundle
2. We found the API configuration (`Ds`, `$e`, `Ze`)
3. We found the decryption chain: `At` (API call) → `Ct` (decrypt) → WASM + Hashids + AES
4. We successfully:
   - Downloaded and analyzed the WASM module
   - Patched the WASM to bypass the verify check
   - Got the WASM decrypt working (first layer produces OpenSSL salted data)
   - Derived the AES key using Hashids + XOR
   - But the final AES decryption still produces garbage

The open question is why AES decryption doesn't work. The issue might be with key size (AES-128 vs AES-256) or EVP_BytesToKey implementation details.

Let me write a comprehensive summary.

## Goal
- Reverse-engineer VidKing's full API/decryption to resolve stream sources via plain HTTP, removing the chromedp/Chrome dependency.

## Constraints & Preferences
- No Node.js, no Chrome/Chromium — pure HTTP requests only.
- Keep existing provider structure in `cineby.go`.

## Progress
### Done
- Replaced agent-browser with chromedp in `resolveVidKingEmbed` (interim stopgap).
- Located and extracted all 3 source-fetch functions (`zs`, `Vs`, `Rt`) from `VideoPlayer-D_vIdw1z.js`.
- Identified API base URL `https://api.videasy.net` and 4 server endpoints: `mb-flix/sources-with-title`, `cdn/sources-with-title`, `downloader2/sources-with-title`, `1movies/sources-with-title`.
- Mapped the full request parameters: `title`, `mediaType`, `year`, `episodeId`, `seasonId`, `tmdbId`, `imdbId`, `_t` (timestamp).
- Downloaded and analyzed WASM module `/assets/wasm/module1.wasm` (263 KB, AssemblyScript).
- Reversed the decryption chain: **WASM serve() → verify(hash) → decrypt(hex, tmdbId) → Hashids+AES final layer**.
- Extracted the 112 KB obfuscated JS that `serve()` returns.
- Successfully executed the WASM in wazero (Go), applying binary patches to bypass the verify check.
- WASM `decrypt()` produces OpenSSL-salted base64 data (`Salted__` + 8-byte salt + ciphertext).
- Implemented Hashids key derivation (`Us()` equivalent): XOR input `tmdbId+"d486ae1ce6fdbe63b60bd1704541fcf0"` with salt `8c465aa8af6cbfd4c1f91bf0c8d678ba`, convert to hex, Hashids-encode.

### In Progress
- Final AES-CBC decryption produces garbage; root cause not yet identified.
  - Suspected: key-size mismatch (AES-128 vs AES-256) or EVP_BytesToKey derivation differs from CryptoJS's internal implementation.
  - CryptoJS EvpKDF with `keySize: 4` (default for AES Cipher) derives only 128-bit key + 128-bit IV from the MD5 hash chain, but current code derives 256-bit key.

### Blocked
- AES decryption step returns garbage, preventing access to the final JSON (`{sources, subtitles, tmdbId}`).
- Lack of ability to execute the obfuscated `serve()` JS (infinite loops in goja, no Node.js) means the `window.hash` value required for proper WASM verify setup cannot be computed easily.

## Key Decisions
- Using wazero (pure Go WASM runtime) to execute the WASM module instead of browser-based WASM.
- Patching WASM binary to bypass abort checks and force verify success — avoids needing the JS-computed hash.
- Hashids + XOR key derivation implemented in pure Go using `github.com/speps/go-hashids/v2`.

## Next Steps
1. Fix AES-CBC key/IV derivation: match CryptoJS's EvpKDF exactly (test `keySize: 4` → 128-bit key, ensure IV is derived correctly).
2. Verify by running `openssl enc -d -aes-128-cbc -md md5 -pass pass:<hashids_key>` on the first-layer output.
3. Once decryption works, build the complete HTTP-only `resolveVidKingEmbed` in `cineby.go`.
4. Remove chromedp-based fallback.

## Critical Context
- API call: `GET https://api.videasy.net/{endpoint}?title=...&mediaType=...&tmdbId=...&_t=<ts>`
- Decryption layers: `API response (hex string)` → **WASM decrypt(hex, tmdbId)** → `OpenSSL salted base64` → **AES-CBC decrypt** → `JSON with sources[] and subtitles[]`
- WASM abort check at func[73] offset 0x46439 (`global.get 70` + `i32.eqz`); patched to `i32.const 1; i32.eqz` in `/tmp/module1-patched3.wasm`.
- Verify check in func[76] offset 0x47315 (`local.get 1; local.get 0; call 34` → `global.set 70`); patched to `i32.const 1; [nops]; global.set 70`.
- AES key derivation: XOR each char of `tmdbId+"d486ae1ce6fdbe63b60bd1704541fcf0"` with char codes of salt `8c465aa8af6cbfd4c1f91bf0c8d678ba`, then Hashids-encode the hex.
- CryptoJS's `EvpKDF` uses `keySize: 4` (words) = 128 bits for the AES cipher by default — current Go code derives 256-bit key (may be wrong).
- The obfuscated JS from `serve()` cannot execute in goja (infinite anti-debug loops). The `window.hash` it computes is needed for the WASM verify function's setup (functions 39–58), but the patch bypasses the check while still running setup.

3. Looking at the CryptoJS source code, the OpenSSL KDF `execute` function generates `keySize + ivSize` words of data, then splits them: first `keySize` words go to key, next `ivSize` words go to IV.

4. For AES with default settings (keySize=4, ivSize=4), this means generating 8 words = 32 bytes of data from EvpKDF, then splitting into 16-byte key and 16-byte IV.
